*else
  *comment Fallback once every rumor above has been heard: the weather talk, built from the same forecast the Weigh House
  *comment posts on the Civic Heights board (weather_tomorrow, forecast_str, tomorrow_severity, forecast_missed), with
  *comment the old warmed-over line one visit in three. Clock math keeps it from repeating back to back.
  *temp tv_fb (((campaign_day * 24) + clock_hour) modulo 3)
  *if (tv_fb = 2)
    The same talk, warmed over and passed around again. Maret draws you a short measure you didn't pay for and doesn't explain it. Some nights, that's the whole of what a taproom is for.
  *elseif (tomorrow_severity >= 3)
    At the next table, two skiff hands are working out whose boat comes up the slip first. "Weigh House slip has ${forecast_str} coming tomorrow," one says, and the other sucks his teeth and drains his cup.
  *elseif (forecast_missed and (tv_fb = 0))
    A drayman scowls at the window and the weather beyond it. "The slip said different," he says to nobody in particular. "The Weigh House gets it wrong often enough, and it always picks my load."
  *elseif (weather_tomorrow = "Fog")
    An old man with rope-scarred hands taps the table twice. "Weigh House has ${forecast_str} for tomorrow," he says. "Nobody wants the channel marks in that."
  *else
    An old man with rope-scarred hands taps the table twice. "Weigh House slip says ${forecast_str} tomorrow," he says, "and it is right more often than it isn't. Ask anyone with a cargo on the water."
  *page_break The talk moves on…
  *goto pv_poi_tavern_menu

*comment ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
*comment THE WEIGH HOUSE
*comment Added 2026-09-24. Port Valen's central trading house, on the cargo quay between the taverns and the customs
*comment tower. The Council keeps the standard weights and measures here and the Gilded Scales' brokers work the rail;
*comment a Scales seal skips the weighing at the gates because the load is weighed here. Customs (Voss, the tower) owns
*comment hulls, papers and duties; the Weigh House owns the goods: weighing, tally, price boards, storage. Fresh fish
*comment never crosses this floor (the Fishmongers' Slip has its own market). The house keeps the weather slate by the
*comment door and issues the forecast slip on the Civic Heights dispatch board, because wet goods weigh heavier.
*comment Hours: open Morning through Afternoon, INCLUDING severe weather (traders come in out of it); shut at Dusk,
*comment Night, Pre-Dawn and Hallowday. The name is learned from the carved lintel (wh_named), so the hub button stays
*comment unnamed until then. Weighmaster Odo Farrow keeps the great scale.
*comment TRADE HOOK: not built. The future trading system lives here: buying and selling commodities by the cargo from a
*comment wagon or cart (Farrow: "bring me a load on a cart"), storage rental, and the price boards, whose figures should
*comment read from the trading system. Goods that already exist in the world and belong on the boards: coal, timber,
*comment pig iron, shear-steel, highland wool, salted cod, hemp, pitch and flax canvas. The weekly cargo rhythm below
*comment (Greyday timber and coal, Tideday foreign freight, Marketday salted barrels, Forgeday iron, Ironday canvas and
*comment hemp) is the natural supply calendar. Lorebook: weigh_house, odo_farrow.
*comment ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

*temp wh_first false
*if (not(wh_seen))
  *set wh_first true
*set wh_seen true
*temp wh_roll 1
*rand wh_roll 1 3
*if (not(wh_named))
  *set wh_named true
  You read the carved lettering over the cart doors as you go in: [i]"The Weigh House. One Weight for All."[/i]

[b]◈ THE WEIGH HOUSE[/b]
*line_break
*comment LAYER 1: PLACE. Constant geography and smell.
Inside the cart doors, a long hall of grey flagstone runs under a high raftered roof. A great iron beam-scale hangs over the central bay on a chain as thick as a wrist, its pans wide enough to take a loaded barrow, and platform scales are sunk flush in the floor along both sides. Racks of squat brass weights stand along the walls beside rows of numbered bins. At the far end a railed gallery overlooks the floor, with a wall of black boards ruled in columns behind it. The air smells of chalk dust, tar, hemp, and cold stone.

*comment LAYER 2: SKY. Weather and light only.
*if (weather_severity >= 3)
  The cart doors are pulled to against the weather with one wicket left open, and the wind moans through the roof timbers while every lamp on the floor is lit.
*elseif (weather = "Rain")
  Rain drums on the roof, and the flags inside the doors are dark with wet boots.
*elseif (weather = "Fog")
  Fog drifts in through the cart doors and clings to the chains of the great scale.
*elseif ((weather = "Snow") or (weather = "Sleet"))
  Slush is tracked across the flags from the doors, and lamps burn along the walls in the grey light.
*elseif (weather = "Clear")
  Long bars of daylight fall through the cart doors and the high windows onto the scale floor.
*else
  A flat grey light comes through the high windows, and lamps burn along the walls.

*comment ── WEATHER MEMORY (calendar ground_state, strictly no people) ──
*if ((ground_state = "sodden") and (not(weather_wet)))
  After days of rain, runoff still trickles in under the cart doors, and the flags near them are slick with old mud.
*elseif ((ground_state = "wet") and (not(weather_wet)))
  The flags by the cart doors are still dark from the last rain.
*gosub pv_wh_slate

*comment LAYER 3: STREET. The weekly cargo rhythm, then the hour.
*if (day_of_week = "Greyday")
  Sawn beams and sacks of coal are on the scales today, and black dust hangs over the bays.
*elseif (day_of_week = "Tideday")
  Bales in unfamiliar cord are stacked at the doors, waiting their turn on the platform after the flood tide.
*elseif (day_of_week = "Marketday")
  Carts of salted barrels are queued out through the doors, and the floor smells of brine.
*elseif (day_of_week = "Forgeday")
  Bars of pig iron and bundles of steel blanks are stacked beside the platform scales, and each one rings when it is dropped.
*elseif (day_of_week = "Ironday")
  Bolts of flax canvas and coils of hemp rope are heaped along the walls.
*else
  *comment Hearthday
  The floor is slow today, with only a few carts at the platform scales.
*if (time_period = "Morning")
  *if (wh_roll = 1)
    Carts queue through the doors in a line that reaches out onto the quay, and a boy on a ladder wipes the boards clean and starts chalking them fresh.
  *elseif (wh_roll = 2)
    The floor rings with weights set down on the platform scales, and men and women in river-serpent livery take their places along the gallery rail one by one.
  *else
    A cart is backed onto the great scale while its owner and a man in city grey check the pans and call the figure across the floor.
*elseif (time_period = "Midday")
  *if (wh_roll = 1)
    The floor is packed. Figures are shouted across the hall from the gallery rail to the platform scales, and the boards are rubbed out and rechalked almost as fast as they are called.
  *elseif (wh_roll = 2)
    A woman in a mud-stained cloak and a man in river-serpent livery haggle at the gallery rail while a boy waits below them with a wet rag.
  *else
    Weights clatter into a bin, a cart-wheel squeals, and the great scale swings and settles under a load of barrels.
*else
  *comment Afternoon
  *if (wh_roll = 1)
    The floor has thinned to a few late carts, and someone at the rail is chalking the day's last figures into a book.
  *elseif (wh_roll = 2)
    Weights are lifted back onto their racks one by one, and a boy sweeps chalk dust toward the doors.
  *else
    A last cart rolls onto the great scale as a man in an apron holds the wicket for it, and the pans settle with a groan of chain.

*if (wh_first)
  On a low platform beside the great scale stands a stout, bald man in a grey coat and a leather apron, a brass plumb-bob on a cord at his belt and chalk to the elbows. He looks up as you come in. "Farrow," he says. "Odo Farrow, Weighmaster. Nothing leaves this floor unweighed, and nothing on it is weighed twice."
*else
  Weighmaster Farrow stands on his platform beside the great scale.

*label pv_poi_quays

  *set death_cause "starvation"

    *set death_cause "exhaustion"

*if (not(pv_quays_seen))
  *set pv_quays_seen true
  *set codex_port_watch true
  *set port_watch_rep +1
  The cargo quay runs from the taverns by the fish market toward the customs tower and the gate where the road down from the terraces comes in, and at this hour it is one long argument conducted in stamped parchment. Guild clerks in the river-serpent colors of the Gilded Scales move crate to crate with wax tablets, while Port Watch officers walk the line with cudgels slung but visible.

  Above it all, standing atop an upturned cargo crate with a brass seal pinned to his salt-crusted oilskin coat, a hawk-nosed man with ink-stained thumbs calls hull numbers down to a runner.

  *if (equipped_cloak_id = "none")
    Dockmaster Harl Voss pauses mid-column as you approach, his sharp grey eyes flicking over your ${armor_prose} and the road-dust on your shoulders.
  *else
    Dockmaster Harl Voss pauses mid-column as you approach, his sharp grey eyes flicking over your ${armor_prose} and the travel-wear on your ${cloak_prose}.

  *if ((equipped_ring1_id = "toll_seal_ring") or (equipped_ring2_id = "toll_seal_ring"))
    His quill halts an inch above the vellum. His eyes narrow on your knuckles, locking onto the tarnished three-headed imperial hawk stamped into your signet ring.

    "That's a Meridian toll-stamp," he mutters, lowering his voice below the clatter of the draymen. "Haven't seen that imperial die since the upper garrison dissolved. Don't go flashing that in front of the Gilded Scales factors, mercenary. They don't like reminders of old crown charters."
  *else
    "Quayside's crowded," he grunts, wiping his pen against his leather apron. "If you're looking to haul crates, talk to the gang-boss by crane three. If you've got business with the harbor registry, wait your turn behind the draymen. Weighing and prices are down the quay at the Weigh House, and that queue is longer than mine."
*else
  *if ((time_period = "Night") or (time_period = "Pre-Dawn"))
    The wooden registry box stands dark and empty under the sea-mist. Above the quayside, a lone lantern burns in the customs tower window where Voss audits tomorrow's tide manifests behind closed shutters. Down on the wet flagstones, a Port Watch sentry in a thick wool greatcoat watches the gate.
    "Quayside's shut down till dawn bell," the sentry mutters, breath misting in the cold. "Gate's closed to traffic till morning."
  *elseif (time_period = "Dusk")
    The day's freight traffic is winding down beneath the customs arches. Voss stands under a dripping oilcloth awning, sealing the last clearance manifests with red wax while his runners padlock the cargo sheds for the night.
    He catches your eye and gives a brisk nod from beneath his cowl before returning to his manifests.
  *elseif ((weather_severity >= 3) or (weather_cold))
    With the weather blowing hard off the bay, all cargo unloading has been lashed down under storm tarpaulins. Voss stands in the lee of the wharf gate, shouting orders through the gale as dray-crews double-rope the river barges against the surging swell.
  *else
    Voss is atop his raised rostrum crate, book chained to his belt, calling hull numbers down to a runner while porters haul crates under the watchful eye of Port watchmen.
    He glances your way without breaking rhythm, one more face in a roster that never stops growing.

*goto pv_poi_quays_menu

*label pv_quays_observe
*comment The cargo line as it now works (2026-09-24): loads come off the barges at the sills, get ticked off, roll on carts to the wide doors of the Weigh House (unnamed until wh_named), and the hull slips go to the customs tower. What comes off follows the same weekday rhythm as the Weigh House floor (Greyday timber and coal, Tideday foreign freight, Marketday salted cod, Forgeday iron, Ironday canvas and hemp). Timeless: only continuous activity, no single person doing a one-off act.
*temp qo_cargo ""
*if (day_of_week = "Greyday")
  *set qo_cargo "Sawn beams and sacks of coal come off the timber rafts and coal barges"
*elseif (day_of_week = "Tideday")
  *set qo_cargo "Bales in unfamiliar cord come off the deep-draft hulls that rode the flood in"
*elseif (day_of_week = "Marketday")
  *set qo_cargo "Barrels of salted cod come off the barges"
*elseif (day_of_week = "Forgeday")
  *set qo_cargo "Bars of pig iron and bundles of steel blanks come off the barges"
*elseif (day_of_week = "Ironday")
  *set qo_cargo "Bolts of flax canvas and coils of hemp rope come off the barges"
*else
  *set qo_cargo "Only a few loads of highland wool come off the barges"
You lean against a mooring bitt and watch the rhythm of the cargo line.

*if (weather_severity >= 3)
  Along the stone quay sills, the river barges have been doubled-roped and stripped of their cargo, and they lunge against the fenders with every surge. The ramps are chained up, the tally boards have gone indoors, and only a Port Watch sentry in a streaming greatcoat holds the gate.
  *if (day_of_week != "Hallowday")
    The wide doors of @{wh_named the Weigh House|the long grey hall under the iron beam-scale} are the one place on the quay still open, with lamplight showing inside.
*else
  Along the stone quay sills, flat-bottomed river barges bump against heavy hemp fenders. ${qo_cargo}, and dockworkers muscle them up slick wooden ramps onto carts, their boots finding purchase in the wet grit. Men and women in river-serpent colors tick each load off on a wax tablet as it passes, and the loaded carts roll along the quay toward the wide doors of @{wh_named the Weigh House|the long grey hall under the iron beam-scale}, while a steady stream of people carry slips up the steps of the customs tower at the far end. Port Watch sentries keep a measured, watchful perimeter.

*if (weather_severity >= 3)
  Spray comes over the quay edge in sheets, and the wind flattens the tarpaulins against the lashed stacks.
*elseif (weather = "Rain")
  Rain drums a steady beat against oiled tarpaulins stretched over open holds, and porters curse as wet crate-ropes slick their palms.
*elseif (weather = "Fog")
  Thick river-mist blurs the crane booms into grey gallows-frames, and the muffled clank of chains and shouts carry eerily over the water.
*elseif ((weather = "Snow") or (weather = "Sleet"))
  Slush cakes the iron wheel rims of the dray carts, and breath plumes in white bursts from the shouting draymen.
*elseif (weather = "Clear")
  The daylight catches the wet sheen of the river and the bright brass seals pinned to the clerks' coats.
*else
  The salt smell of the estuary mingles with coal smoke and damp canvas beneath the overcast sky.

*page_break Back to the cargo quays…
*goto pv_poi_quays_menu

*label pv_quays_voss_reminder
You step up to the customs desk while Voss is dipping his steel nib. He looks up, giving your kit a brief, assessing scan.

"Silt-Gate drop is in Dredge-End, remember," Voss reminds you in a low murmur. "Peak flood tide only clears the masonry culvert late at [b]night or pre-dawn[/b]. Keep your eyes on the water."
*page_break Return to the quayside…
*goto pv_poi_quays_menu

*label pv_quays_voss_chat
You step up to the harbor desk where Dockmaster Voss is reviewing the week's river registrations. He nods as you approach, his tone curt but professional.

*if ((kestrel_slip_turned_in) and (campaign_day >= (silt_gate_turned_in_day + 3)))
  *set silt_gate_voss_told true
  "Odd thing," he says, without looking up. "Sergeant Kray used to look straight through me. Eleven years. This week he's saluted me twice and asked after my health." He dips his nib. "The far bank's no better watched than it was. But the men on it have turned polite, and in my experience that means someone's frightened them."

  He blots the line. "I've decided not to ask who. Keep your ears open, stranger."
*else
  "Tides are running high, and the Patrician Quarter is pressing for faster clearances," he says, checking an inked entry. "Watch has its hands full keeping the quays open. Keep your steel sheathed and your ears open, stranger."
*page_break Return to the quayside…
*goto pv_poi_quays_menu

*label pv_quays_voss_briefing
You step up to the lee of the customs tower where Dockmaster Voss is arguing with a sweating watchman over a discrepancy on a cargo manifest. When you draw near, Voss waves the watchman off with a flick of his ink-stained fingers and turns his attention to you.

He looks you over: the lack of a city tabard, the worn field kit, and the hard, capable way you carry yourself on the crowded wharves.

"You don't look like a dock-porter," Voss says, setting his pen in an inkwell. "And you aren't wearing city colors. You looking for paid work, or just taking up dry ground?"

*choice
  # "I'm looking for coin, and you look like a man with more problems than hands to handle them."
    *goto pv_quays_voss_pitch
  # "Just a traveler passing through the lower quarter. If you have work that pays fair and doesn't ask my life story, I'm listening."
    *goto pv_quays_voss_pitch
  # "I'm offering seasoned muscle and craft. What's got the Watch in a twist?"
    *goto pv_quays_voss_pitch

*label pv_quays_voss_pitch
Voss gives a dry, humorless snort and leans over his counter, lowering his voice against the noise of the cranes.

"Highland tool-steel and untaxed mountain liquor," he says, tapping an inked line on his parchment. "It's showing up in taprooms and back-alley smithies throughout the lower quarter, and none of it came over my piers."

He points with his quill downriver toward the low, leaning roofs of Dredge-End.

"Our outer boom chains are locked tight, so the cargo isn't coming through the main channel. It's slipping into the canal drainage tunnels under the slums: the Silt-Gates. Every time I get the Watch to send a patrol into the canals, rooftop lookouts blow tin whistles and the boatmen dump the crates into ten feet of river sludge before they get within three hundred paces."

His jaw tightens. "Worse, half the night sergeants working this stretch take weekly hush-money to walk their beats on the far side of the canal when the tide rises."

"A flat-bottomed boat can only clear the low stone drain when the tide is at full flood, which means the drop only happens late at [b]night or pre-dawn[/b]. Get down into [b]Dredge-End[/b], scout the channel, and see what's moving through it."

*choice
  # "I'll scout the channel before the tide rises and wait for the drop."
    "Keep your eyes open," Voss grunts, blotting his ink. "Dredge-End swallows people who move slow."
    *set silt_gate_quest_stage "active"
    *goto pv_poi_quays_menu
  # "If your night sergeants are dirty, you'll need their names to clean up the roster."
    Voss's eyes narrow, but he doesn't dispute it. "If you catch anyone with a Watch badge taking silver from a canal runner, bring me proof or names. I'll make sure the Council hears about it."
    *set silt_gate_quest_stage "active"
    *goto pv_poi_quays_menu
  # "Twelve silver marks if the cargo is intact. What if the smugglers put up a fight down in the mud?"
    "The city pays for cargo in coin and paper, not corpses in the canal," Voss replies flatly. "Keep it quiet. I'd rather the town criers heard nothing."
    *set silt_gate_quest_stage "active"
    *goto pv_poi_quays_menu

*if (not(crane_seen))

  Crane three stands out from the rest of the cargo line before you're close enough to hear it: the boom's swung half over the hold and stopped there, chain gone taut and silent, a crate dangling in midair over the slip with nobody minding it. At the foot of the gangplank, four dockers stand shoulder to shoulder with their arms folded, watching the mud instead of the ship.

  A heavyset man in a salt-stiffened canvas coat has climbed halfway up the plank to shout at a figure on the rail above, a wax-slated manifest board tucked under one arm like a shield. "…and I don't care whose coin it was," he's saying, "it wasn't mine, and it isn't costing me my contract."

  He drops down onto the flagstones, sees you watching, and doesn't bother pretending he wasn't just losing an argument.

  "You're not one of mine," he says, sizing up your kit in a single pass. "Dockmaster send you down, or are you just here for the show?"
*else
  *if (crane_job_unlocked) and (crane_last_shift_day = campaign_day)

    *elseif (crane_full_pick = 2)

*label pv_crane_dockers
You cross to the dockers instead. The nearest of them, a rangy woman with rope-scarred forearms, doesn't uncross her arms, but she does look at you instead of the mud.

"You're not crew," she says. "Rilla. I speak for the crew, and I'll say this once. That ship's last run, the first mate paid them short — full day on the slate, clipped coin in the hand."

"The Scales won't hear a word against their own factor, so we're not touching that hull again till somebody makes it right."

"Dell took the hire anyway." She tips her chin at the plank. "If it burns him, that's not on us. We never took it."

*label pv_crane_offer
*if (crane_grievance_known)
  "You've talked to Rilla's lot, then," Dell says, before you've asked anything. "So you know why I'm short four hands and a headcount I can't fill by the bell."
*else
  "Dell," he says. "I run crane three." He jerks a thumb at the dockers behind him. "Half my crew's standing at the foot of that plank refusing to touch the hold. One more's laid up since a sling parted under a load yesterday. That leaves me two hands short of what the factor's contract needs cleared before the evening bell, or he docks my pay for every hour past it. My pay, not the city's."

He looks you over again, more carefully this time.

"You're not one of theirs, you're not bound by whatever's got them standing on principle today. That's worth something to me right now."

"Forty-odd crates in that hold. Three coppers for the shift, same as I pay any hand off the street, and three more on top if it's cleared and dry before the bell. You in?"

*label pv_crane_shift_intro
*comment Real combinatorial variety for a job meant to be replayed dozens of
*comment times: familiarity tier (crane_shifts_completed) x a *rand pick
*comment within that tier x weather (all 8 states) x day_of_week (all 7) x
*comment time_period. Nothing here changes mechanics -- pure flavor, same
*comment principle as the district ambient descriptions, just crossed with
*comment more axes since this location gets revisited far more than any of
*comment those ever do in one playthrough.
*temp crane_greet_pick 0
*if (not(crane_job_unlocked))
  Dell walks you down to the open cargo hatch himself, counts crates with a jabbing finger, and hands off a spare chalk-board, not bothering to check if you can read one.
*elseif (crane_shifts_completed < 3)
  *rand crane_greet_pick 1 3
  *if (crane_greet_pick = 1)
    Dell waves you over with barely a glance up from his board, still sizing you up the way he does anyone who hasn't proven themselves ten times over. "Berth's this way."
  *elseif (crane_greet_pick = 2)
    Dell spots you at the edge of the crane's shadow and jerks his chin toward the lower deck, the gesture of a man who hasn't decided yet whether you're worth remembering.
  *else
    Dell's already mid-complaint about the morning's manifest when you walk up, and only breaks off to point you at the cargo bay once he notices you're actually listening.
*elseif (crane_shifts_completed < 10)
  *rand crane_greet_pick 1 3
  *if (crane_greet_pick = 1)
    Dell waves you over to whatever hull's tied up short-handed today and points out the freight hatch with little ceremony. By now neither of you needs the introduction.
  *elseif (crane_greet_pick = 2)
    "There you are," Dell says, like you're a fixture of the crew now rather than a stranger off the street, and folds you straight into the count mid-stride.
  *else
    Dell absorbs you directly into the rhythm already running: a nod, a jerk of the thumb toward the barge hold, and he returns to his count before you've finished walking over.
*else
  *rand crane_greet_pick 1 3
  *if (crane_greet_pick = 1)
    Dell's saved you the easiest berth before you've even asked: dry crates, an easy count, the kind of load reserved for hands he knows well.
  *elseif (crane_greet_pick = 2)
    "Figured you'd be by," Dell says, already walking, and skips explaining the job entirely, expecting you to know your part.
  *else
    One of the regular dockers claps you on the shoulder on the way past, the way you greet somebody who stopped being new a long time ago, while Dell merely marks his board, aware of your arrival without looking up.

*if (weather = "Clear")
  The sky's stayed clear the whole shift, and the crane gang's making the most of it: no tarps, no delays, just steady lifts under open sky.
*elseif (weather = "Overcast")
  A flat grey sky sits over the wharf, the dull kind of light that makes every hour of a shift look the same as the last.
*elseif (weather = "Rain")
  Steady rain slicks the flagstones under the crane, and every crate coming off the sling arrives a shade heavier for the rain it's soaked up.
*elseif (weather = "Storm")
  A hard wind drives rain sideways off the bay, and the crane gang works in short, hunched bursts between gusts strong enough to twist a sling off true.
*elseif (weather = "Fog")
  Thick fog's rolled in off the estuary, swallowing the far end of the pier whole. Dell keeps the lifts slow and calls out each arc before it happens.
*elseif (weather = "Snow")
  Snow settles thin and quick over the stacked crates, and somebody's detailed to sweep the crane's footing clear before every lift.
*elseif (weather = "Sleet")
  Sleet needles down sideways, stinging any bare skin, and the whole team works with their collars up and their tempers short.
*else
  *comment Blizzard
  The blizzard's driven half the waterfront indoors, and the few crates still moving get muscled by hand rather than trust the crane's boom in this wind.

*if (day_of_week = "Ironday")
  It's an ordinary working day along the docks, everything is running steady and nobody in any particular hurry.
*elseif (day_of_week = "Tideday")
  The flood tide has every shift on the slipways racing to clear their cargo before the water climbs the pilings, crane three included.
*elseif (day_of_week = "Marketday")
  Marketday's doubled the crowd at every gangplank, and Dell wants this load shut fast just to keep the crane's radius clear of the foot traffic.
*elseif (day_of_week = "Hearthday")
  Half the regular crews have traded a shift for the hearth-fires at home, which is exactly why Dell's short-handed enough to need you.
*elseif (day_of_week = "Forgeday")
  The Iron Wharves are running full blast today, and the ring of caulking hammers carries all the way down to crane three.
*elseif (day_of_week = "Greyday")
  Greyday's thick river damp has everything running slick and slow, and Dell's hands move with the particular caution of people who've all slipped on these boards before.
*else
  *comment Hallowday
  The church bells carry down from Civic Heights even here, and the waterfront runs quieter than usual for it: fewer hands, fewer words, the work getting done all the same.

*if (time_period = "Morning")
  The morning shift is still shaking off the night, and the crane's first lifts of the day come slow until everyone's properly awake.
*elseif (time_period = "Midday")
  It's the thick of the midday shift, cranes swinging in a steady rhythm up and down the whole quay.
*elseif (time_period = "Afternoon")
  The afternoon sun's baked the flagstones dry, and the crew's settled into the unhurried rhythm of a shift with hours still left in it.
*else
  *comment Dusk
  The light's going, and Dell wants this hold cleared before the lamps have to come out. Nobody likes working a crane swing by torchlight.

*if (check_success)
  You stop matching the crew's pace and start setting it, taking the heaviest crates off the sling two at a time and walking them clear before the next load's even swung over. By the time the last one hits the boards, the dockers at the foot of the plank have stopped pretending not to watch.

  Dell inspects the cargo belly, checks the sky, and glances below decks again. "Dry, counted, and early," he says, like he doesn't quite believe his own count. "Fine work."
  *set crane_shift_ontime true
  *set crane_shift_damaged false
*else
  You keep pace by refusing to slow down, and the barge clears on schedule, but somewhere in the last dozen crates, something in your shoulder gives with a wrench you feel all the way to your teeth. You finish the lift anyway, because stopping halfway through a crate is worse than finishing it wrong.
  *if (not(stat_bump_locked)) or (not(locked_stat_bump_page_id = page_id))

    *gosub_scene calendar recalc_hp_from_neglect

*label pv_crane_shift_dex
*set hours_to_pass 4
*set minutes_to_pass 0

*set crane_first_shift_method "rigging"
*gosub_scene dice_engine roll_skill_check 12 "acrobatics" "" "" "" "" ""
*if (check_success)
  You take the guy-lines instead of a crate and start calling the swing yourself: a beat faster on the pickup, a beat slower on the drop, timed to land every load dead over its stack instead of a shuffle short. The crew catches the rhythm before they've worked out where it came from, and the hold empties in a run of clean, unhurried lifts.

  Dell watches the last crate settle without a single crack of wood against flagstone and doesn't try to hide being impressed. "Dry, counted, and nobody's back is going to hurt for it tomorrow. That's worth the coin on its own."
  *set crane_shift_ontime true
  *set crane_shift_damaged false
*else
  You call a swing wrong, and a crate comes in low and wide, clipping the coping stone hard enough to crack a slat. The crew freezes; Dell doesn't. He's already crouched over the damage, prying the lid open to check what's underneath.

  It's glazed pottery, half of it in pieces. Dell swears once, thoroughly, and resumes counting the rest of the hold.
  *set crane_shift_ontime true
  *set crane_shift_damaged true
*goto pv_crane_shift_resolve


*label pv_crane_shift_int
*set hours_to_pass 3
*set minutes_to_pass 45
*set time_advance_call_id "pv_crane_shift_int_1"
*gosub_scene calendar advance_time

*if (hp_current <= 0)
  *set death_cause "starvation"
  *if (neglect_damage_fatigue > neglect_damage_hunger)
    *set death_cause "exhaustion"
  *goto_scene death death_screen

*set crane_first_shift_method "tally"
*gosub_scene dice_engine roll_skill_check 11 "investigation" "" "" "" "" ""
*if (check_success)
  You take Dell's chalk-board instead of a crate and read it the way you'd read a supply manifest before a march: not what's in the hold, but what order it has to come out in so nobody's walking a load past a load already moving. You call the new sequence once, and the crew, short-handed as it is, stops retracing their steps within three lifts.

  The hold clears faster than its own headcount should allow. Dell looks at the empty deck, then at the board in your hand, and doesn't ask to reclaim it right away.

  "Should've thought of that myself," he says, which is as close as he comes to a compliment.
  *set crane_shift_ontime true
  *set crane_shift_damaged false
*else
  Your new order looks right in chalk and falls apart the moment it meets a crew that already knows the old one in their hands. Barely into the second lift, someone's blocking someone else's path to the stack, and you spend the better part of half an hour unraveling your own sequence before the hold's clear.

  Dell checks the hold, checks the sun, and doesn't check his temper. "Counted. Late. I don't pay bonus on late."
  *set crane_shift_ontime false
  *set crane_shift_damaged false
*goto pv_crane_shift_resolve


*label pv_crane_shift_cha
*set hours_to_pass 4
*set minutes_to_pass 40
*set time_advance_call_id "pv_crane_shift_cha_1"

*if (hp_current <= 0)
  *set death_cause "starvation"

    *set death_cause "exhaustion"
  *goto_scene death death_screen

*set crane_first_shift_method "talked"
*gosub_scene dice_engine roll_skill_check 11 "persuasion" "" "" "" "" ""
*if (check_success)
  You climb the plank before you've touched a single crate and put the demurrage clock to the mate directly, not as a threat, just arithmetic: a rushed crew drops things, a dropped crate costs the factor more than an hour's grace would, and word of a shorted crew doesn't help him tie up here again either. The mate doesn't care for it, but he doesn't argue with the math either.

  "Two hours," he says, "and not a minute past it."

  With the bell no longer breathing down anyone's neck, the cargo bay clears at an ordinary working pace: nobody rushing, nothing dropped.
  *set crane_shift_ontime true
  *set crane_shift_damaged false
*else
  The mate looks down at you from the rail like you've asked him to pay twice for the same cargo. "The bell rings when it rings," he says, and turns back to his cargo rolls without another word.

  You climb down having spent the better part of an hour for nothing, and the barge still has to clear on the original clock. The crew clears it eventually, working past the bell's toll by the time the last crate's down.
  *set crane_shift_ontime false
  *set crane_shift_damaged false

*if (crane_shift_ontime)
  Dell inspects the empty deck against his manifest, and against the sky. "Dry, counted, and early. I don't know what you said to him, and I don't want to."
*else
  Dell inspects the empty belly. "Counted. Dry. Late enough the factor'll hear about it. Don't spend an hour talking next time. Spend it lifting."
*goto pv_crane_shift_resolve

*if (crane_quest_stage = "active")

  *comment crane_quest_stage became "resolved" just above. Re-run it now that the dependency changed.
  *gosub_scene calendar update_hub_condition
  Dell counts your coppers into your palm directly, keeping his board balanced against his hip.

  "Half my crew won't touch this hull, one's laid up, and the factor doesn't care whose fault that is," he says. "If you're still looking for honest pay some mornings, come find crane three. I can't promise every day, but most of them."
*else
  *temp crane_close_pick 0
  *if (crane_shifts_completed < 3)
    *rand crane_close_pick 1 2
    *if (crane_close_pick = 1)
      Dell presses the payment into your palm and returns to his figures with only a brisk nod, routine, for both of you now.
    *else
      Dell pays you out to the copper, checks the count twice out of habit, and resumes his board in silence.
  *elseif (crane_shifts_completed < 10)
    *rand crane_close_pick 1 2
    *if (crane_close_pick = 1)
      Dell hands off your wages without looking up from his work, the coppers already sorted before you'd finished the last lift.
    *else
      "Same time tomorrow, if you're around," Dell says, handing your wages over plainly, already thinking about the next hull.
  *else
    *rand crane_close_pick 1 2
    *if (crane_close_pick = 1)
      Dell dispenses with counting out loud, just presses the coppers into your palm and resumes his work, the way you pay someone you trust.
    *else
      One of the regular dockers already has your share set aside before Dell's even reached the pay box, which tells you plainly enough what the crew thinks of you now.

*page_break Back up to the quayside…
*goto pv_poi_quays_menu


*label pv_poi_drydock
*set hours_to_pass 0
*set minutes_to_pass 10
*set time_advance_call_id "pv_poi_drydock_1"
*gosub_scene calendar advance_time
*gosub_scene calendar recalc_hp_from_neglect

*if (hp_current <= 0)
  *set death_cause "starvation"
  *if (neglect_damage_fatigue > neglect_damage_hunger)
    *set death_cause "exhaustion"
  *goto_scene death death_screen

*temp drydock_closed false
*if ((((time_period = "Dusk") or (time_period = "Night")) or (time_period = "Pre-Dawn")) or (weather_severity >= 3))
  *set drydock_closed true

*if (not(pv_drydock_seen))
  *set pv_drydock_seen true
  *set gilded_scales_rep +1
  Beyond the customs tower the seawall turns to shipyard wall, and the Iron Wharves announce themselves before you even clear the gateway: coal smoke, showers of forge-sparks, and the rhythmic ring of caulking mauls drifting across the basin. Behind the fortified seawall, the shipyard ways climb out of the tide like the ribbed skeletons of great beasts: river barges and ocean-going hulls stripped down to the frames, steamed planks going down seam by seam beneath the Gilded Scales' river-serpent standard.
*else
  The Iron Wharves again: the sharp tang of boiled pine pitch, coal smoke, and the relentless chime of irons along the building cradles.

*comment LAYER 2: SKY & LIGHT AT THE SLIPWAYS
*if ((time_period = "Night") or (time_period = "Pre-Dawn"))
  *if (weather_severity >= 3)
    The shipyard is black and shuttered against the howling squall. Out in the open basin, breakers smash against the granite sills of the docks, and all work along the gantries is dead. Inside the vaulted gate-alcove, a solitary yard sentry in a streaming oilskin coat shelters a single horn lantern from the driving sea.
  *else
    *if (weather = "Rain")
      Rain trickles through the dark scaffolding, hissing faintly where stray drops hit the warm chimney of a forge shed. Out on the cradles, lantern baskets cast long, trembling shadows across the wet gravel.
    *elseif (weather = "Fog")
      Cold river fog wraps the slipways in deadening stillness, muffling the lap of the tide against the timber flood-gates.
    *elseif ((weather = "Snow") or (weather = "Sleet"))
      A crust of pale slush coats the dark cradles and scaffolding, and the iron fittings wear a skin of brittle frost.
    *else
      *comment Clear / Overcast
      The main ways are dark and barred beneath the seawall, the tall gantry arms standing like gaunt gallows against the star-dusted night sky.

  *comment Nighttime weekly rhythms (Infrastructure and security)
  *if (day_of_week = "Forgeday")
    The covered smithies still glow with banked embers from the day shift, the smell of burnt oil hanging thick under the eaves.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Tideday")
    The midnight spring tide rises to its highest mark, dark seawater lapping the lower granite launch sills.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Hallowday")
    The slipways are quiet under curfew, save for a solitary tallow candle flickering inside an iron shrine-box beside the timber gate.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Marketday")
    Empty lumber wagons and flatbed drays stand parked in long rows outside the timber yards, awaiting the morning deliveries.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Greyday")
    Moored timber rafts and coal scows ride low in the channel, their riding lanterns rocking slowly on the water.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Ironday")
    A stiff offshore breeze whistles through the bare rigging of the refit berths, halyards slapping rhythmically against pine spars.
  *else
    *comment Hearthday
    The wharf gates are locked tight, and the yard rests in undisturbed silence.

*elseif (time_period = "Dusk")
  *if (weather_severity >= 3)
    The dusk bell tolls early through the gale. Shipwrights battle the rising squall to double-chain the drydock flood-gates and lash loose lumber to the granite retaining wall before darkness covers the basin.
  *else
    The dusk bell clangs down the ways. Shipwrights douse their charcoal hearths with damp sand, pack augers and broadaxes into heavy oak tool-chests, and stream toward the quayside gates while watchmen padlock the seasoning sheds.
    *if (weather = "Rain")
      Lanterns sputter under dripping eaves as workers pull oilskin mantles over their shoulders to face the wet trudge inland.
    *elseif (weather = "Fog")
      Torches glow like blurred amber halos through the creeping river mist, craftsmen calling out warnings as they navigate the slick gangways.

  *comment Dusk weekly rhythms
  *if (day_of_week = "Forgeday")
    Apprentice strikers finish banking the forge hearths with wet turf, the day's final showers of orange sparks dying out over the water.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Tideday")
    Crews hurry along the granite catwalks to secure the drydock flood-gates before the evening tide reaches full flood.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Hallowday")
    Distant chapel bells echo from the upper city, and shipwrights rinse pitch and sawdust from their hands at the tidal troughs before turning home.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Marketday")
    Carters haul away empty timber-skids and barrel hoops, clearing the main haul-lanes for tomorrow's work.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Greyday")
    Lumber crews finish securing fresh log rafts against the river pilings, checking the chain binders against the evening swell.
  *elseif (day_of_week = "Ironday")
    Riggers bundle canvas tarpaulins and coil spare mooring hawsers into covered storage racks for the night.
  *else
    *comment Hearthday
    The yard empties well before dusk, the tool houses locked and the slipways settling into quiet rest.

*else
  *comment Daytime (Morning, Midday, Afternoon)
  *if (weather_severity >= 3)
    With the gale howling off the estuary, all crane hoisting has ground to a halt. Balance derricks are chained fast to the granite wharves, and carpenters work beneath covered sheds, re-lashing storm tarpaulins and securing half-framed hulls against the surging basin.
  *else
    *if (weather = "Rain")
      Driving rain sheets off the gantry booms and drums against overturned wooden hulls, hissing where stray drops strike the boiling pitch cauldrons. Caulkers work beneath sagging canvas lean-tos, driving greased oakum into steaming seam lines with sodden mallets.
    *elseif (weather = "Fog")
      Cold river fog rolls off the estuary, swallowing crane arms and tall masts in murky grey. The clink of chisels and caulking irons rings out from unseen scaffolding, echoing through the muffled gloom.
    *elseif ((weather = "Snow") or (weather = "Sleet"))
      Freezing slush crusts the scaffolding, and slipway apprentices shovel coarse salt along the walkways while charcoal braziers glow cherry-red beside every hull to keep irons hot.
    *elseif (weather = "Clear")
      Sharp daylight glints off bronze fastening spikes and freshly planed spruce planks, pine resin and hot black lacquer scenting the crisp sea air.
    *else
      *comment Overcast
      A leaden sky hangs over the shipyard gantries, the smell of coal smoke, steamed cedar, and bubbling pitch thick along the seawall.

    *comment Weather memory at the slipways
    *if ((ground_state = "sodden") and (not(weather_wet)))
      After days of rain, the sawpit trenches stand full of dark water, and the slipway gravel is a slick morass of soaked sawdust and clay.
    *elseif ((ground_state = "wet") and (not(weather_wet)))
      The launching slides and staging planks remain dark and slick from recent rain.

    *comment LAYER 3: SYSTEMIC SHIPYARD LABOR
    The slipways run at a steady, deafening roar. Balance cranes swing massive oak balks overhead on groaning chains, pit-saws rasp through highland timber, and shaping-axes notch curved frames with splintering thuds.
    *if (day_of_week = "Forgeday")
      Forgeday pushes the metal trades to their peak: shipyard hearths roar under leather bellows, and smiths hammer hull braces, rudder brackets, and mooring rings in blinding showers of sparks.
    *elseif (day_of_week = "Tideday")
      High tide brings launch and docking operations to the forefront; crews grease launching ways with rendered tallow and monitor the stone flood-gates as the water peaks against the sills.
    *elseif (day_of_week = "Marketday")
      Dray wagons crowd the wharf gate, unloading barrels of Stockholm tar, sacks of cured hemp oakum, and crates of iron spikes bought at the morning auctions.
    *elseif (day_of_week = "Hallowday")
      Work is noticeably subdued for chapel observance; heavy sledges are quiet, and carpenters work at lighter bench tasks—planing molding strips and checking framing joints.
    *elseif (day_of_week = "Greyday")
      Raw timber handling dominates the yard: teams of draft oxen drag massive unhewn oak trunks off river rafts into the sawpits to be ripped into hull planks.
    *elseif (day_of_week = "Ironday")
      The working week opens with heavy assembly: carpenters fit curved knees into newly framed hulls, and riggers inspect tackle blocks along the upper staging.
    *else
      *comment Hearthday
      A reduced maintenance pace settles over the cradles; crews grease crane gears and clean out pitch troughs while apprentices sweep shavings from the haul-ways.

    *comment Dynamic hourly shipyard vignette: changes every hour
    *temp drydock_vignette (((campaign_day * 24) + clock_hour) modulo 6) + 1
    *if (drydock_vignette = 1)
      [i]Two apprentices trudge past carrying a smoking cauldron of black pitch on a wooden yoke, warning bystanders aside with sharp shouts.[/i]
    *elseif (drydock_vignette = 2)
      [i]Down on the staging, a shipwright swings a broad curved adze with rhythmic precision, shaving pale ribbons of spruce from a massive deck beam.[/i]
    *elseif (drydock_vignette = 3)
      [i]An overhead tackle block screeches in protest as a four-man derrick crew strains against a hemp hawser, hoisting an oak knee into position.[/i]
    *elseif (drydock_vignette = 4)
      [i]Beside a partially planked hull, a caulker works down an open seam, driving tarred hemp deep between the strakes with sharp, ringing mallet taps.[/i]
    *elseif (drydock_vignette = 5)
      [i]A pair of mud-caked draft oxen heave against heavy iron chains, dragging a massive highland trunk across the gravel toward the sawpit skid.[/i]
    *else
      [i]Near the bending sheds, two carpenters use heavy iron clamps to force a steamed oak rib against a framing template, steam billowing into the cool air.[/i]

*if (drydock_closed)
  The slipways are closed to casual traffic at this hour, so you turn back toward the upper quayside.
  *page_break Back up to the quayside…
  *goto port_valen_harbor_pois

  *if (weather_severity >= 3)
    A gust with real weight behind it rips a tarp off the sawpit frames, and a whistle shrills twice along the ways. Foremen wave everyone down off the staging, and by the time you reach the gate a watchman is already hauling the chain through the bars.
  *else
    A watchman comes along the ways with a lantern and a ring of keys, waving the last stragglers toward the gate while crews smother their hearths under wet sand. Behind him the yard falls quiet, tarps are hauled over the frames, and the pitch kettles cool ticking on their tripods.
  *page_break Back up to the quayside…
  *goto port_valen_harbor_pois

*if (time_period = "Morning")
  *if (dry_pick = 1)
    The pitch kettles are still coming up to a boil along the ways, and the crews are working through their first hard hours. Steam curls off the plank-bending trenches, and the foremen walk the slips checking the morning's seams.
  *elseif (dry_pick = 2)
    The ways are still cold enough to smell of tar and fresh-cut cedar. Pitch kettles tick toward a boil, mallets start up slow along the slips, and the foremen stalk between the cradles reading the night's damp off the timbers.
  *else
    First shift is at it hard. Adzes bite into green oak, apprentices run water to the steaming trenches, and the pitch kettles come up to a boil while the gantries creak under the morning's first loads.
*elseif (time_period = "Midday")
  *if (dry_pick = 1)
    Saws and mallets keep a steady beat down the slips, and half the crews are eating on their feet between strokes. Heels of bread and cold fish go from hand to hand, and nobody puts down a tool for long.
  *elseif (dry_pick = 2)
    The noon yard is all rhythm: mallets, saws and the hiss of steam from the bending trenches. Crews eat standing, with bread in one fist and an adze in the other, and the foremen bellow the break over almost before it starts.
  *else
    Midday finds the shipwrights in full swing. Caulking irons chatter along a hull, sawdust hangs above the sawpit, and crews wolf their bread and fish between strokes rather than lose the tide of work.
*else
  *if (dry_pick = 1)
    The crews have been at it long enough that the mallets have slowed to a plod, and shavings lie ankle-deep under the frames. Sweat has dried to salt on every neck, and the foremen have stopped shouting and started counting the hours.
  *elseif (dry_pick = 2)
    Afternoon has worn the yard down. Mallets plod, apprentices drag brooms through shavings piled ankle-deep under the frames, and the gantry crews rest on their hands between lifts, listening for the shift bell.
  *else
    The long afternoon grinds along the ways. Caulking mauls fall out of rhythm, shavings drift into the corners under the frames, and every foreman has taken to glancing toward the gate where the watch will soon come with the chain.
*if (weather = "Rain")
  Rain drums on the plank roofs of the seasoning sheds and beads on every tarred surface.
*elseif (weather = "Fog")
  Fog blurs the far slips into grey shapes, and every mallet blow reaches you a beat late.
*elseif ((weather = "Snow") or (weather = "Sleet"))
  Slush freezes into ridges between the skids, and crews scatter coarse salt ahead of their boots.
*elseif (weather = "Clear")
  Bright light picks out every resin bead and bronze fastening along the hulls.
*else
  Flat grey light lies over the ways, and coal smoke hangs low above the forge sheds.

*choice

*label pv_poi_drydock_observe
You lean against the stone retaining wall above the ways and watch the yard work. Balance cranes swing heavy oak timbers through the air, weighed down by iron cages of river stone. Shipwrights drive boiling tar into hull seams with steady mallet strikes, and foremen with chalkboards shout numbers over the rasp of the sawpits. Every hull on the ways has a crew's name chalked on its cradle and a fine written under it.
*page_break Back to the shipyard lane…
*goto pv_poi_drydock_menu

*label pv_poi_drydock_salvage
You walk the granite gate walkway above the lower basin. Heavy timber flood-gates hold the river silt back from the dry cradles, and scrap bins sit banked along the wall, heaped with seasoned oak wedges, curled pine shavings, and scrap iron pulled from broken keels. Down below, the river water slaps against the stone sills, scouring silt and green river slime off the launching slides.
*page_break Back to the shipyard lane…

*comment ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
*comment BEAT 1: THE DISCOVERY (SLIPWAY TWO)
*comment The shipwright stays "the old man" in narration until he gives his
*comment own name in beat_2_reveal_rot. The apprentices name him first, so the
*comment player has heard it before he says it.
*comment ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

*label beat_1_discovery
*set brant_saw_him true
The air down on the shipwrights' ways is heavier than on the upper quays. It is thick with coal smoke from the forge hearths, boiling pine tar, and the sour green smell of steamed spruce. Across the basin, caulking irons ring against the planking of a pair of cargo vessels on the neighboring slips, and sawpit crews shout over the rasp of the frame-saws.

On the slipway, the ribs of a seventy-foot merchant ship rise out of the granite cradle like the picked bones of a sea monster. Down in the mud beneath the keel, a pair of young apprentices sweep cedar shavings into wicker baskets and stack split oak offcuts. Up in the ribs, working alone in the half-light under the deck beams where the yard noise is muffled by curved frames, an old man in a tar-stained leather apron moves along the frames with a short-handled iron mallet.

[i]Clink.[/i] The mallet strikes the third rib, and the note rings clear and long in the oak.

He steps forward a few paces.

[i]Thud.[/i]

The fourth rib answers dull and wet, like a fist hitting a sack of turnips.

The old man lowers the mallet to a cross-beam. He drags a blunt thumb across the black pitch wash painted over the wood and brings the thumb to his nose. He pauses, breathes out slowly, and rests his forehead against the damp frame, his shoulders sagging as if forty years of work had settled into them all at once.

*choice
  # Step onto the scaffold planking to see what the old man found in the hull.
    *goto beat_2_shipwright
  # Watch from the lumber skids and see how the old man and his apprentices work.
    *goto beat_1_observe
  # Mind your own business and step back to the shipyard lane.
    *goto pv_poi_drydock_menu

*label beat_1_observe
*set met_hendryk true
You lean against a stack of spruce planks and watch the ship from the gravel lane. Down by the keel, the apprentices huddle over their broom handles and keep their voices low beneath the clatter of mallets.

"He's been at that one rib since the shift started," the taller boy murmurs, glancing up. "There's wet coming through the black paint. Good wood doesn't do that."

"If he stops the work, Master Hendryk docks our pay," the younger boy mutters, kicking a wood shaving into the mud. "If he doesn't, she falls apart the first time she meets a big wave."

Up on the staging, the old man stays pressed against the fourth rib, glaring at the dark paint with quiet, smoldering fury.

*choice
  # Step onto the scaffold planking to speak with him.
    *goto beat_2_shipwright
  # Approach the apprentices sweeping shavings by the keel.
    *goto beat_1_ask_apprentices
  # Leave the old man to his troubles and return to the shipyard lane.
    *goto pv_poi_drydock_menu

*label beat_1_ask_apprentices
*set met_brant true
You step down into the mud beneath the ship's overhang, where the young apprentices are packing cedar curls into wicker hampers. When your shadow falls across their work, both boys stiffen and clutch their brooms.

"We're not holding up the ways, sir," the taller boy stammers, wiping wood dust off his nose with a greasy cuff. "Master Brant's just checking the framing. If you're from the front office, he's right up on the scaffold."

"Don't look like a factor's clerk," the younger boy mutters, nudging a cedar wedge with his toe. "Clerks don't get mud on their boots."

Neither boy seems to know anything beyond his broom handle and his fear of the factor's fines.

*choice
  # Climb the wooden ladder to the scaffold planking to speak with Brant.
    *goto beat_2_shipwright
  # Step back to the shipyard lane.
    *goto pv_poi_drydock_menu

*comment ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
*comment BEAT 2: APPROACHING THE SHIPWRIGHT
*comment beat_2_decision is the only place rotten_rib_quest_stage becomes
*comment "active" and the splinter is handed over.
*comment ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

*label beat_2_shipwright
*set met_brant true
When your boots hit the scaffold planking, the old man keeps his hand flat against the fourth frame, feeling the grain.

"Watch the grease on the launching slides," he grunts, his voice gravelly from decades of sawdust and estuary damp. "Slip on that ledge and you'll slide thirty feet into the mud, and nobody fishes you out till the tide turns."

He wipes his fingers on his apron, leaving a dark smear of pitch, and turns to face you. His beard is grey as driftwood, his forearms crosshatched with old tool scars, and his eyes are watery and pale under deep crow's-feet caked with charcoal dust. He takes in your stance with the wariness of a man who has been lied to by better-dressed people than you.

"You don't look like one of Hendryk's clerks," he says flatly. "This is a working cradle, and staging ropes fray without warning down here. What brings you onto my scaffolding?"

*choice
  # "That second strike didn't ring like the first."
    *goto beat_2_examine_rib
  # "Looking for work along the wharves, or for someone who knows where the coin moves."
    *goto beat_2_mercenary_work
  # "My mistake. I'll leave you to your work."
    *goto beat_2_leave

*label beat_2_examine_rib
The old man's bushy grey eyebrows twitch. He studies your face, checking it for mockery and finding none.

"You've got an ear on you, then," he murmurs.

*label beat_2_reveal_rot
*set met_hendryk true
He taps the fourth rib with his knuckles.

"Listen to that," he says.

He taps the third rib again, and it gives back a clean iron ring. Then the fourth, and it gives back the flat, sodden thud.

"Third rib is highland heartwood," he says. "Felled in deep winter three years back, air-cured on river skids, hard enough to turn an arrowhead. Fourth rib smells of damp bark and summer sap. Mountain spruce, green as a cabbage. Barely cured a month."

He scratches at the dark coating with a calloused thumbnail. Under the tarry stain the wood is pale and wet, weeping tiny beads of sticky sap.

"Painted over with black pitch to pass for heartwood," he says. "Stamped with the Gilded Scales' inspection mark right where the rib meets the keel. Once this hull takes twenty tons of tallow and salt cod into open swell, the water will twist her. Green wood doesn't flex, it splits. The caulking will pop like old shoe stitching, and she'll open up from the keel in four fathoms of water."

He looks toward the yard gates, where a clerk with an armful of papers argues with a team of draymen.

"Thirty souls on her crew," he says, wiping pitch from his thumb. "And Brant's name is carved into her keel-block. She's booked to launch on the next flood tide the guild will sign for. Hendryk fines me ten silver a day for every tide this cradle sits occupied past the contract date. If I pull these frames, I stop the work. If I stop the work, the guild docks my bond, throws my apprentices into the street, and leaves me forty years in debt. If I don't pull them, thirty people drink harbor water before harvest."

*choice
  # "Who stamped the inspection mark? Where was the load delivered?"
    *goto beat_2_inquire_trail

*label beat_2_inquire_trail
Brant points a calloused thumb at a faint red wax impression pressed into the wood near the keel line.

"The yard clerk cleared it," he grunts. "The manifest said three long beams of cured highland oak went onto rack four behind the sawpits. If real heartwood was delivered, it was there this morning. Go look at the rack if you've a mind to."
*goto beat_2_decision

*label beat_2_bargain_reward
Brant gives a dry chuckle and wipes his pitch-stained hand on his apron. He understands the question. Coin-hunters don't work for prayers.

"I've got no guild silver. Hendryk keeps my bond in an iron vice," he says plainly. "But I've forty years of craftsman's credit and a chest of prime bull-hide. I can build you a pair of iron-heeled shipwright boots that hold on any grease, scaffold, or wet deck in this basin. And as long as my slipway stands, you'll have a friend on these ways."

He nods toward the seasoning sheds. "The delivery was signed for rack four behind the sawpits. Start there."
*goto beat_2_decision

*label beat_2_suggest_factor
Brant shakes his head, his mouth setting into a hard line.

"Hendryk wants paper," he says. "If I walk into his office with nothing but a wet splinter, the clerk pulls out his signed delivery sheet, swears I ruined the wood myself, and Hendryk docks my bond on the spot. In this yard paper beats wood every time. If we want the factor to listen, we have to find where the real heartwood went."

He looks back toward the sheds. "Check rack four behind the sawpits. That's where the paper says my heartwood was stacked."
*goto beat_2_decision

*label beat_2_decision
*set rotten_rib_quest_stage "active"
*set met_elric true
*set has_rotten_rib_splinter true
Brant draws a small hand-chisel from his apron pocket. With a practiced strike of his palm he shears a thick, sap-weeping splinter off the fourth rib, catches it in his other hand, and presses the pale, sticky wood into your fingers.

"Take this," he grunts, his eyes steady on yours. "Smell that raw mountain sap? Keep it in your pouch. If anyone claims Bay Four delivered cured heartwood, put this under his nose. The one you'll be dealing with is Elric, the clerk who runs the yard office up on the stilts behind the sawpits. Every load in this yard gets his initials. Do your looking before he moves that oak. In this yard, whatever sits still too long grows legs."

*choice
  # Head uphill through the yard lane to the Seasoning Sheds and Sawpits right now.

*label beat_2_identify_heartwood
Brant taps the edge of his hand-chisel against the third rib.

"Three things," he grunts, holding up three calloused fingers. "First, grain rings: tight, dark bands, thirty to the inch. Highland oak grows slow up in the high cols. Second, weight. Cured heartwood feels like cold pig-iron when you lift one end. Third, my brand. My yard mark is a square notch over crossed adzes, branded deep into the butt-end of every beam cleared for Slipway Two. If they shaved that mark off, the fresh cut will still smell of pine oil and iron."

He nods toward the seasoning sheds. "Now go, before it's gone."

*choice
  # Head uphill to the Seasoning Sheds and Sawpits now.
    *goto beat_3_investigation
  # "Understood." Step off the staging back to the shipyard lane.
    *goto pv_poi_drydock_menu

*label beat_2_shipwright_checkin
Brant is still working in the hull's shadow, checking the pitch wash on the third frame with a worn rasp. He looks up as your boots hit the staging.

"Still framing this green spruce," he grunts. "Did you find where they moved the highland oak from rack four? Look behind the sawpits before Elric gets it onto the river."

Brant runs a thumb down the fourth rib, shaking his head with a low grunt.

"A pair of Hendryk's gate runners walked the cradle," he murmurs, his voice guarded. "Pretended to check the tidal gates, but their eyes were counting my frames. The factor knows this hull is late. If you're going to turn up that heartwood, don't dawdle."

*label beat_2_decline
Brant turns his back to you, digs an iron brace out of his chest, and begins bolting scrap metal across the rotten spruce, reinforcing wood that cannot be saved.
*page_break Back to the shipyard lane…
*goto pv_poi_drydock_menu

*label beat_2_mercenary_work
The old man snorts and shakes his head. "Coin? Nobody makes coin here but the factor and the river brokers. Unless you can haul logs or swing a broadaxe for ten hours without dropping it, there's no day-wage on these ways."

He pauses with his hand on the fourth rib, jaw tightening as he glares at the damp wood.

"Unless you're the sort who knows how to run down thieves. Look here." He strikes the frame with his knuckles, and it answers with a dull, wet thud. "Listen to that."
*goto beat_2_reveal_rot

You give the old man a short nod and make your way back down the slipway slides to the shipyard lane. He stays in the ribbed shadow with his palm pressed against the flawed frame.

*comment ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

You head uphill through the yard lane toward the seasoning sheds. The smell of estuary mud gives way to the resinous bite of fresh shavings, damp bark, and chimney smoke.

The seasoning yard sits under an open-sided pole shed roofed with woven reed mats to keep rain off the drying lumber. Trunks of highland oak and mountain spruce rest on greased skids. In the middle of the lane, a pair of sawyers stand in a seven-foot pit, pulling a long frame saw through an eight-foot oak trunk. Yellow sawdust showers over their sweating shoulders with every rasping stroke. Directly above the pit, perched on stout wooden stilts, sits the yard clerk's office, its shuttered window looking out over the lumber rows.

*temp tried_yard_ledger false
You make your way past the sawpits to the numbered storage bays along the north wall and count over to the fourth.

*label beat_3_investigation_hub
Pinned to the oak pillar of Bay Four under a rusted iron spike is a delivery manifest bearing the red wax seal of the Gilded Scales: [i]Three hewn beams, prime highland heartwood, seventy feet, cleared for Slipway Two.[/i]

Beneath the parchment, Bay Four's skids sit bare. Crushed bark and damp impressions in the gravel mark where long logs rested until someone dragged them off.

You crouch beside Bay Four. In the packed gravel and damp sawdust the trail is unmistakable: three heavy beams were dragged off the skids with iron drag-hooks and wooden rollers.

The drag-scrapes bypass the lane to Brant's slipway. Instead the ruts cut south, skirting the back of the sawpits toward a brick boiler shed, where coal smoke from the pitch vats rolls low across the ground and screens the corner from view.

You follow the gouges through the smoke, staying low and out of sight.
*goto beat_3_find_stash

*label beat_3_track_mud_failure
You crouch by Bay Four and try to pick the heavy drag gouges out from the rest. But dray hooves and hundreds of yard boots have churned the ground to soup. You spend several minutes following an old iron-scrap furrow toward the slag heaps.

Turning back through swirling pitch smoke, your boot strikes a discarded pry-bar with a sharp clang. The smoke thins and leaves you in plain view of a worker tending the tar vats. He glares and shouts a warning up toward the raised office: "Hoy! Watch the vats, stranger!"

Up in the clerk's office, a shutter swings open. You spot a canvas tarp behind a stack of cod crates, but your approach was noisy.
*set elric_alerted true

You run a finger down the chalk delivery notes scrawled on the pillar beside the spike. The wagon driver logged the delivery under Team Three when the oak cleared. Right beside the driver's mark, a second hand has initialed [i]E.V.[/i] and reassigned the team to the western river-gate under a "special transit waybill," with a note for the boiler shed.

The oak never went down to the cradle. Someone sent it straight to the river wall.

You walk over to the rim of the pit. The rhythmic rasp of the long saw slows, then stops. The sawyer at the rim wipes sweat from his brow with a forearm and looks up, breathing hard, while his partner leans against the dirt wall down in the trench.

"Looking for the Bay Four oak," you say, pitching your voice over the hiss of the boiling tar. "Who hauled it out?"

The sawyer spits dark tobacco into the sawdust and wipes his mouth with the back of his hand, eyes narrowing at you. "We're paid to saw oak, stranger, not answer questions for drifters. If you want yard business, take it to Hendryk's guards at the gate. Clear off before a beam rolls."

The sawyer spits into the sawdust, eyes darting toward the raised office above before settling back on you.

You keep to the shadows of the stacked log skids and move toward the western wall, where the yard opens onto a muddy river-gate. Out in the channel, private barges take on scrap iron and salvaged rope under tarpaulins, far from the customs tower.

Near the gate, a pair of rough-looking barge hands lounge against stacks of salted-cod barrels, sharing a clay pipe and keeping a casual eye on the lane. Behind them, tucked into the dead space between the boiler chimney and the stone retaining wall, sits a massive heap draped in weathered storm-canvas.

*label beat_3_find_stash
*set has_diverted_timber_waybill true
Behind the brick boiler shed, hidden under stacks of salted cod crates and a heavy oiled storm-tarp, lie three massive, silver-grey beams of highland oak.

You pull back the stiff canvas. The wood is dense, cool, and dry to the touch, and smells faintly of old forest humus and winter frost. A square notch over crossed adzes is branded into each butt-end. Wet white chalk scrawls a new destination across the grain:

[i]Barge Osprey, Dredge-End Cut.[/i]

Tacked to the underside of the oilcloth with an iron nail is a folded document, a private transit waybill in the clerk's hand. It carries a red wax seal stamped with the scales-and-compass mark of Factor Hendryk and the Gilded Scales, and scribed ink falsely condemning the highland oak for "worm-rot" and clearing it for disposal downriver.

You fold the waybill into a tight square and slip it into your pouch. Someone skimmed thirty silver marks' worth of naval-grade heartwood to sell to a private refit in Dredge-End, and swapped in rotten spruce to cover the theft.

*if (elric_alerted)
  Above the lane, yellow candlelight flickers erratically in the small window of the wooden office on stilts. Through the dirty panes you can see the clerk pacing behind his desk with a heavy brass scale-weight in his grip, while a pair of burly draymen linger at the foot of his stairs.
*else
  Above the lane, yellow candlelight burns steady in the small window of the wooden office on stilts. The clerk sits alone with his quill scratching across open paper, unaware of you.

You lower the stiff canvas over the beams, wedge the cod crates back into place, and take the long way around the sawpits, keeping the lumber stacks between you and the office window.

*label beat_3_brant_report
Brant sees you coming across the staging and lays his rasp down on the cross-beam. You keep your voice low and tell him about the tarp behind the boiler shed, the notched butt-ends, and the waybill bearing Hendryk's stamp.

The old shipwright listens with his eyes shut and his palm on the fourth rib. When you finish, he lets out a long breath through his nose.

"That's my oak," he says. "Every stick of it." He opens his eyes. "Listen close. Hendryk won't take a stranger's word over his own clerk's, but he'd sooner die than have anyone misuse his personal stamp. That waybill frightens the factor worse than any splinter. Elric's the opposite: a coward with a pair of draymen and a lockbox. Push him hard enough and he folds, but he'll only fold for something he's afraid of. Fear of Hendryk will do it. So will fear of a bailiff's iron."

He picks his rasp back up. "What you do with it is yours to decide. Just don't let that oak reach the river."

*comment ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

*label beat_4_climb
*set hours_to_pass 0
*set minutes_to_pass 10
*set time_advance_call_id "rotten_rib_climb_1"
*gosub_scene calendar advance_time
*gosub_scene calendar recalc_hp_from_neglect

*if (hp_current <= 0)
  *set death_cause "starvation"
  *if (neglect_damage_fatigue > neglect_damage_hunger)
    *set death_cause "exhaustion"
  *goto_scene death death_screen

You cross the lane under the sawpit rasp and start up the wooden stairs, the treads flexing under your weight.
*goto beat_4_clerk

*label beat_4_clerk
The clerk sits in a cramped wooden office built on stilts above the sawpits. The room smells of dry ink, cold mutton grease, and pipe smoke. Behind him, tall pigeonholes hold rolled paper orders. When you step through the doorframe, the floorboards creak under your boots.

*if (elric_alerted)
  Elric stands behind his desk with his arms crossed over his chest, his narrow jaw tight and his face pale. Ink stains his cuffs to the wrist. An iron lockbox sits under his stool, shut and padlocked. At the foot of the exterior stairs, a pair of heavy-shouldered draymen lean against the railing with clubs in hand, watching the door.

  "I heard the commotion down by the skids," Elric says coldly, his fingers tapping a heavy brass scale-weight beside his inkpot. "Master Hendryk's guards are one whistle away. Whatever you're selling, I'm not buying."

  You pull Brant's sap-weeping splinter from your pouch and drop it onto the desk. A sticky bead of resin smears across the polished oak.
*else
  Elric looks up from his desk, quill poised over an open daybook. He is young and sharp-featured, with fingers stained purple around the nails. His eyes flick to your belt, then back to your face.

  "All yard labor requisitions go through Master Hendryk's front office," he says smoothly, dipping his quill. "If you want day-haul wages, the line forms at the outer gate. We don't hire off the street here."

  You draw Brant's sap-weeping splinter from your pouch and lay it across his open daybook, right over the fresh ink. A sticky bead of resin smears across the column.

The quill stops. Elric stares at the wet wood for three long seconds. The office goes quiet, apart from the [i]rasp-rasp-rasp[/i] of the pit-saw working under the floorboards.

"What is that?" he asks, his voice dropping.

"That's the fourth rib of the ship down on Brant's slip," you say. "Fresh spruce. Green as spring grass. Painted over in the dark with pitch."

Elric lays his quill in the pewter stand and leans back on his stool, folding his ink-stained hands over his stomach.

"Wood cures differently depending on the valley it was felled in," he says, perfectly flat. "The wood was surveyed, weighed, and stamped by the harbor inspector. The papers are filed with the Gilded Scales."

"The papers say three heavy beams of highland heartwood went to Brant's slip," you answer. "The heartwood is sitting behind the boiler shed under an oilcloth, chalked for the Osprey and Dredge-End. And I have your waybill with Hendryk's personal stamp on it, right here in my pouch."

Elric's jaw tightens. A nerve jumps under his left eye. He looks out the square window toward the slipways.

"You don't know this quarter," he says, very low. "You think you've caught a thief. You think there's an honest piece of wood in this whole basin. The factor knows that ship is insured through the guild's lenders. If she sinks in deep water, the guild collects the bond and the builder's deposit. Nobody asks about the framing once the water's over the masthead."

*if (elric_alerted)
  He takes a small cloth purse from his vest and sets it heavily on the desk.

  "Four silver marks," Elric says, his eyes flicking toward the window where his draymen wait. "Take it, walk out the front gate, and forget the sound of Brant's mallet. Push this further, and my draymen will throw you into the basin mud before you reach the factor."
*else
  He takes a small cloth purse from his leather vest and sets it on the desk. It clinks with the muffled ring of silver.

  "Eight silver marks," Elric says. "That's more than a month of honest wages. Walk back out the gate, forget the sound of Brant's mallet, and let the ship launch."

*label beat_4_intellect_fail
Elric recovers his footing, his face hardening as he leans across the desk. "You know charter law, stranger, but you don't know who owns the bailiffs. Hendryk pays their wages." He raps the brass weight twice against the desk, and boots pound up the stairs.

Two heavy-shouldered draymen fill the doorway. "Gurn. Corve," Elric says, almost pleasantly. "This one is lost. See him to the lane." They take an arm each and walk you down the stairs and along the gravel until the yard gate is in sight, then let go with a shove that puts you on your knees in the sawdust.

"Bring that paper up here again," Elric calls from the landing, "and it'll be the Watch waiting for you."
*goto beat_4_strike

*label beat_4_intimidation_fail
Elric flinches as the desk shudders, but his eyes slide past you to the door, and the flinch turns into a smile. "You can break my desk, stranger. Gurn and Corve break necks for a living." He puts two fingers in his mouth and whistles.

The draymen take the stairs three at a time. You get one hand on the nearer man's sleeve before the other's forearm bars your throat, and then the pair of them are hustling you down the steps and along the lane with your boots barely touching the gravel, until they fling you against the yard fence hard enough to knock the wind out of you.
*goto beat_4_strike

*label beat_4_blackmail_fail
Elric's mouth thins into a venomous line. He leans back and rests both hands flat on his desk.

"You're waving paper you don't understand," he whispers. "Hendryk knows every piece of heartwood that moves in this yard. Take that to him alone, and you'll learn who the Watch arrests for extortion." He nods once toward the window.

The draymen come up the stairs without hurry. They walk you out between them the way you walk a drunk home, one hand under each elbow, and set you down in the lane with a friendly pat that has a good deal of weight behind it.
*goto beat_4_strike

You push the silver purse firmly aside.

"Keep your coin," you say.

Elric blinks. "What do you want, then?"

"The three highland oak beams behind the boiler shed go back to Brant's slipway before that barge ever ties up," you say, your voice even. "Brant gets his oak, and his apprentices start fitting ribs the moment it lands. If a single splinter goes missing between the shed and the slip, we have another conversation."

Elric swallows, his eyes darting to your belt. "And the waybill? You give me the slip?"

You rest a hand against your belt pouch and feel the stiff parchment bearing Master Hendryk's personal stamp.

"I keep the paper."

Elric's face goes the color of curdled milk. He scrambles half off his stool. "You can't do that! Who are you selling it to? Who do you answer to? The harbor bailiffs? The city inspectors?"

You let the silence hang and let his fear do the work. A clerk who used his master's proof-stamp to condemn naval oak under city charter knows the penalty. His eyes dart between your belt and the door as if he can already hear the iron locks of the debtor hulks swinging shut. As long as that stamped waybill exists in unnamed hands, Elric will never know when the hammer falls.

*if (elric_alerted)
  At the foot of the stairs, the draymen step forward. You hold up the red-waxed waybill so Hendryk's seal catches the daylight. "Factor's audit," you say flatly. "Lay a hand on me and you join your clerk in the lockup before the shift bell." The draymen glance at the stamp, exchange an uneasy look, and step back.

Down on the slipway, a yard horn sounds across the water. Before the steam has cleared from the sawpits, four workers appear rolling the three dark highland oak beams down the skids onto Brant's slip.

Brant watches the yard crane lower the dense oak into the ship's framing cradle. He looks across at you, his rough face softening with relief. He reaches into a heavy wood chest and brings out a pair of thick-soled bull-hide boots, sealed with black pitch against harbor mud and fitted with iron heel-plates.

"I don't know what you said to that snake on the stilts," Brant says quietly, handing you the boots with a firm grip. "And I won't ask. But this cradle remembers who pulled her out of the mud. If you ever need honest counsel, word along the wharves, or a skipper who doesn't ask the guild's leave, my slip is open to you."
